I keep steering clear of my own workshop entirely, and I won't go near any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be. \n\nWhich is a real shame, because I used to love our family cookouts. My sister Martha, she makes this incredible potato salad. The secret is she mixes a little bit of dill pickle juice right into the mayonnaise. Most folks use sweet relish, but Martha swears by the dill. She got the recipe from our mother, who used to make it for the Fourth of July picnics down at Miller's Pond. We'd pack up the old Chevy station wagon—had that fake wood paneling on the sides, you know the kind—and drive down there early to get a good picnic table near the water. You had to get there before 8 AM or the Miller family would take up all the good spots under the big oak trees. They had this massive canvas tent they'd pitch every single year, took 'em about three hours just to tie the thing down to the stakes... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The get-togethers. Yeah, I just... after all this time, I'm just tired of planning my whole life around not running into those reminders. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares about the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Nightmares? Well, I suppose so. Most nights I can get to bed just fine, but then I'll wake up from a bad dream about the workshop. Or I'll just be lying there, listening for noises in the house. It's an older house, you know, built in '54, so it settles a lot at night. We bought it from a fellow named Arthur Pendelton. He was a retired conductor for the B&O Railroad. Nicest guy you'd ever meet. He had this massive model train set down in the basement when we moved in. Took up half the room! O-gauge, I think it was. He had little trees, a mountain made of papier-mâché, and a tiny diner that actually lit up from the inside. My nephew Jimmy used to love playing with trains when he was a boy. He's an accountant now down in Tampa. Terrible humidity down there, I don't know how he stands it. I visited him in August once, stepped right out of the airport and my glasses fogged up instantly. Couldn't see a darn thing until I got into his air-conditioned Buick... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the nightmares. Right. Yeah, so if I wake up from one of those dreams about the workshop, or if I'm just listening to the house creak, I end up being wide awake for an hour or two. Just staring at the ceiling, waiting to get tired again.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid talking or thinking about the workshop or your brother-in-law when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a few minutes ago, that's really the whole reason I finally came in to see you. I keep completely steering clear of my own workshop, even when I'm just puttering around the house by myself. I try my hardest not to even let my mind go there. I'll turn on the television just for the background noise, you know, to keep my thoughts busy. \n\nI usually put on those old Westerns on channel 43. Gunsmoke is on every afternoon at two o'clock. I read in a magazine once that they had to put the other actors on apple boxes just to get them in the same frame as him without it looking ridiculous. My old buddy Frank was tall like that. Six foot five, played center for our high school basketball team back in '64. We actually went to the state championship that year, if you can believe it. Frank had this old Ford pickup truck, painted robin's egg blue, and we used to ride in the back of it all the way to the county fair every August. They had these deep-fried funnel cakes that were just covered in powdered sugar, you'd take one bite and get it all over your shirt...\n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, my mind wanders. I had a torn meniscus back in '82, fell off a ladder while I was cleaning out the gutters... \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, right, my daily life. And my painting... well, that comes in fits and starts now. I used to paint quite a bit, mostly landscapes, but I avoid the place where I kept most of my supplies now. It's just easier that way, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts, feelings, or conversations related to the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I just mentioned that, didn't I? I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop, and I won't go to any family get-together if there's even a chance he might be there. I'm just... He hated those bugs. Terrified of them! Stayed in a little cabin near Traverse City. They grow the best cherries up there, you know. My wife and I went up there for our twentieth anniversary. Like I said, I'm just sick and tired of having to dodge it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or flashbacks of the argument with your brother-in-law that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about it happening all over again, like I'm standing right there, but... out of nowhere, I'll see his hand holding that heavy tool. Jimmy was wearing these awful bright yellow shorts that summer... \n\nBut, uh, problems with family? I don't know, really. I just kind of keep to myself more. When we first got married, we lived in this little apartment over on Elm Street, and she brought home this stray cat. Orange tabby, missing half an ear. Looked like he'd been through a meat grinder. We called him Barnaby. Best mouser you ever saw, though. One time, Barnaby dragged a mouse right onto the good rug in the living room while my mother-in-law was visiting. Oh, she practically jumped out of her skin! Spilled her tea all over the sofa. It was a floral pattern sofa, very popular back in the seventies, with these big scratchy cushions... \n\nAnyway, what was I... right, the guilt. Yeah, I just keep beating myself up over it. Should have just said no when he asked to stay. Should have been firmer about it.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. Ever since that whole confrontation with him, which was... gosh, about two years and four months ago, I'd say. Time really flies, doesn't it? It feels like just yesterday we were fixing up that workshop. I built all the workbenches myself, you know. Used solid pine. I just keep thinking that maybe none of this would have happened if I'd handled it like a man right from the start. \n\nLike my father used to say, you've got to nip these things in the bud. He was a landscaper, my dad. Worked with his hands every day of his life until the arthritis got too bad. He used to take care of the grounds over at the old courthouse downtown. Beautiful place, had these massive azalea bushes all along the front steps. They bloomed this bright, vibrant pink every spring. Folks would come from two towns over just to take pictures in front of them for prom. I remember my own prom, I wore this awful powder blue tuxedo with a ruffled shirt. Ruffles! Can you imagine? My date was Mary Lou Jenkins. Sweet girl, but she stepped on my feet the whole night. We went to this little diner afterwards that used to be on the corner of Main and 4th. Best milkshakes in the county. They made them with real malt powder, not that artificial stuff they use nowadays. The guy who ran the place, Sal, he had this glass eye that always seemed to be looking just a little bit to the left of you when he talked...\n\nBut anyway, yeah. I think about it constantly. Tommy was built like a brick outhouse even when he was twelve. He grew up and joined the Navy, ended up stationed out in San Diego. Beautiful out there, they say. I've never been past Chicago myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't say it's about a panic attack or... or a physical reaction, really. It's more that I just keep steering clear of my own workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might show up. I'm just so tired of planning my whole life around not running into those reminders, you know? 

Reminders... it's funny how things stick with you. Like the smell of sawdust. I used to love that smell. Reminds me of when I was a kid helping Mr. Henderson build birdhouses. He lived three doors down, had this old golden retriever named Buster who would sleep right under the table saw while it was running. Never bothered the dog one bit. Buster was the friendliest dog, except when the mailman came. The mailman back then was a fella named George. George always wore these little blue shorts, even in November. He said it built character. He actually ended up moving to Florida, said his knees couldn't take the winters anymore. Florida... my wife and I went to Daytona once. The sand there is so hard you can drive a car right on the beach. We rented this big boat of an Oldsmobile, must have been nineteen-seventy-something. The radio only played country music, no matter what dial you turned...

But anyway, yeah, I just... I came in because I'm avoiding the workshop and the family things. I'm just exhausted from trying to dodge it all.

Could carve a mallard duck out of a block of pine like nobody's business. I gave one of those ducks to my nephew, Tommy, back when he was playing shortstop for the high school baseball team. State champions, they were, in... oh, '89, I think it was. We sat in those hot aluminum bleachers all afternoon watching him play. My wife brought this little plaid thermos full of sweet tea. You remember those old metal thermoses with the glass liner inside? If you dropped 'em even once, they'd shatter into a million pieces. Broke my heart when I finally dropped that plaid one on the concrete driveway years later. We had to sweep for an hour to get all the glass up. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, what was the question? But when it pops in my head, I guess my chest gets a little tight. \n\nTight like... well, like the lid on that old pickle jar my wife used to keep on the top shelf of the pantry. She loved those bread and butter pickles. We used to get them from this little farm stand out on Route 9. The fellow who ran it, old man Higgins, he had this big golden retriever named Buster who would just lay on the porch all day long. Sweetest dog you ever met. I always wanted a dog like that, you know? But we lived in that little duplex over on Elm Street for the first ten years of our marriage, and the landlord, Mr. Kravitz, he was dead set against pets. Said they ruined the carpets. Which is funny, because the carpets in that place were this awful mustard yellow shag anyway. You could lose a whole quarter in that carpet and never find it again. I remember dropping my car keys in it once and... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, thinking about the argument. Yeah, I just... Out of nowhere, I'll just... I'll see his hand with that heavy tool again. It's like a picture that just pops into my head, clear as day. Sometimes it happens while I'm shaving, or... or working on a painting. I've taken up watercolors, you know. Mostly landscapes. There's this old barn out on County Road 4 that I've painted three times now. The roof is all caved in on one side, and the wood is this beautiful weathered gray. Reminds me of the siding on my grandfather's house back in Ohio. He built that house himself, you know. Laid every brick of the foundation. He used to sit on the front porch and smoke this cherry pipe tobacco... the smell of it would carry all the way down the street. I haven't smelled tobacco like that in fifty years. Now everyone vapes, or whatever they call it. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right, the picture in my head. Yeah, when I see his hand with that tool, it just... it still makes my chest tighten up. Actually, that's the whole reason I finally came in to see you. It's getting to be too much. I keep steering clear of my own workshop entirely, and I won't go near any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be. \n\nWhich is a real shame, because I used to love our family cookouts. My sister Martha, she makes this incredible potato salad. The secret is she mixes a little bit of dill pickle juice right into the mayonnaise. Most folks use sweet relish, but Martha swears by the dill. She got the recipe from our mother, who used to make it for the Fourth of July picnics down at Miller's Pond. We'd pack up the old Chevy station wagon—had that fake wood paneling on the sides, you know the kind—and drive down there early to get a good picnic table near the water. You had to get there before 8 AM or the Miller family would take up all the good spots under the big oak trees. They had this massive canvas tent they'd pitch every single year, took 'em about three hours just to tie the thing down to the stakes... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The get-togethers. Yeah, I just... after all this time, I'm just tired of planning my whole life around not running into those reminders. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares about the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Nightmares? Well, I suppose so. Most nights I can get to bed just fine, but then I'll wake up from a bad dream about the workshop. Or I'll just be lying there, listening for noises in the house. It's an older house, you know, built in '54, so it settles a lot at night. We bought it from a fellow named Arthur Pendelton. He was a retired conductor for the B&O Railroad. Nicest guy you'd ever meet. He had this massive model train set down in the basement when we moved in. Took up half the room! O-gauge, I think it was. He had little trees, a mountain made of papier-mâché, and a tiny diner that actually lit up from the inside. My nephew Jimmy used to love playing with trains when he was a boy. He's an accountant now down in Tampa. Terrible humidity down there, I don't know how he stands it. I visited him in August once, stepped right out of the airport and my glasses fogged up instantly. Couldn't see a darn thing until I got into his air-conditioned Buick... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the nightmares. Right. Yeah, so if I wake up from one of those dreams about the workshop, or if I'm just listening to the house creak, I end up being wide awake for an hour or two. Just staring at the ceiling, waiting to get tired again.\n\nDoctor: Do you also avoid talking or thinking about the workshop or your brother-in-law when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a few minutes ago, that's really the whole reason I finally came in to see you. I keep completely steering clear of my own workshop, even when I'm just puttering around the house by myself. I try my hardest not to even let my mind go there. I'll turn on the television just for the background noise, you know, to keep my thoughts busy. \n\nI usually put on those old Westerns on channel 43. Gunsmoke is on every afternoon at two o'clock. I read in a magazine once that they had to put the other actors on apple boxes just to get them in the same frame as him without it looking ridiculous. My old buddy Frank was tall like that. Six foot five, played center for our high school basketball team back in '64. We actually went to the state championship that year, if you can believe it. Frank had this old Ford pickup truck, painted robin's egg blue, and we used to ride in the back of it all the way to the county fair every August. They had these deep-fried funnel cakes that were just covered in powdered sugar, you'd take one bite and get it all over your shirt...\n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, my mind wanders. What were we talking about? Oh, being alone and thinking about it. Right. Yeah, I just try to block the whole thing out completely. Like I mentioned before, I won't go near the workshop, I won't go to any family get-together if he might be there... I'm just so tired of planning my everyday life around dodging those reminders. I mean, I still manage the basics just fine. I get up, I make my coffee—Folgers, always Folgers, none of that fancy stuff they sell nowadays. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to buy those whole beans and grind them herself every morning. Sounded like a jet engine taking off in the kitchen at 6 AM. She bought this grinder at a yard sale down on Maple Street. The lady having the sale was moving out to Arizona to be closer to her grandkids. Said the winters here were getting too hard on her joints. I can understand that, my left knee acts up every time it rains. I had a torn meniscus back in '82, fell off a ladder while I was cleaning out the gutters... \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, right, my daily life. Yeah, so I still do the basics, but I've stopped doing my regular volunteer jobs. The ones that involved the workshop or the big family events, anyway. And my painting... well, that comes in fits and starts now. I used to paint quite a bit, mostly landscapes, but I avoid the place where I kept most of my supplies now. It's just easier that way, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding thoughts, feelings, or conversations related to the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I just mentioned that, didn't I? I mean, that's exactly why I made the appointment to come see you. I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop, and I won't go to any family get-together if there's even a chance he might be there. I'm just... I'm so tired of planning my whole life around not running into those reminders. It's exhausting. \n\nJust like when my cousin Eddie used to plan his whole summer around avoiding the cicadas. He hated those bugs. Terrified of them! In '87, when the big brood came out, Eddie actually packed up his station wagon—it was one of those old Ford Country Squires with the fake wood paneling on the side, a real beauty, though the transmission slipped if you took it over fifty—anyway, he packed it up and drove all the way up to northern Michigan just to get away from the buzzing. Stayed in a little cabin near Traverse City. They grow the best cherries up there, you know. My wife and I went up there for our twentieth anniversary. My chest just tightens right up. \n\nI use a safety razor, you know. The kind where you put the double-edged blade in yourself. My father taught me how to shave with one of those when I was fifteen. Those ducks were mean, I tell you! One time, a big mallard chased my cousin Jimmy all the way to the parking lot because Jimmy tried to take back a piece of bread he dropped. When we first got married, we lived in this little apartment over on Elm Street, and she brought home this stray cat. Orange tabby, missing half an ear. Looked like he'd been through a meat grinder. We called him Barnaby. Best mouser you ever saw, though. One time, Barnaby dragged a mouse right onto the good rug in the living room while my mother-in-law was visiting. Oh, she practically jumped out of her skin! Spilled her tea all over the sofa. It was a floral pattern sofa, very popular back in the seventies, with these big scratchy cushions... \n\nAnyway, what was I... right, the guilt. Yeah, I just keep beating myself up over it. Should have just said no when he asked to stay. Should have been firmer about it.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. Ever since that whole confrontation with him, which was... gosh, about two years and four months ago, I'd say. Time really flies, doesn't it? Oh, she practically jumped out of her skin! Spilled her tea all over the sofa. It was a floral pattern sofa, very popular back in the seventies, with these big scratchy cushions... \n\nAnyway, what was I... right, the guilt. Yeah, I just keep beating myself up over it. Should have just said no when he asked to stay. Should have been firmer about it.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been avoiding your workshop and family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. Ever since that whole confrontation with him, which was... gosh, about two years and four months ago, I'd say. Time really flies, doesn't it? It feels like just yesterday we were fixing up that workshop. I built all the workbenches myself, you know. Used solid pine. Picked it up from that lumberyard out on Route 9, the one that burned down back in '98. Or was it '99? Terrible fire. The smoke was so thick you could smell it all the way across town for a week. The fire chief, old Charlie Henderson, he said they pumped water for twelve hours straight. Charlie and I went to high school together. He played defensive tackle on the football team. Big guy, but he had the softest hands, played the piano beautifully. His mother was a music teacher, taught half the kids in the county how to play \"Chopsticks\" on this old upright Baldwin she had in her parlor... \n\nBut, oh, right... the workshop. Yeah, I haven't been down there, or to the family things, for about two years and four months. Ever since that day.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently during the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, I think about it pretty much all the time. It just goes round and round in my head. I keep telling myself I should have put my foot down sooner, you know? Never should have let him stay with us to begin with. I just keep thinking that maybe none of this would have happened if I'd handled it like a man right from the start. \n\nLike my father used to say, you've got to nip these things in the bud. He was a landscaper, my dad. Worked with his hands every day of his life until the arthritis got too bad. He used to take care of the grounds over at the old courthouse downtown. Beautiful place, had these massive azalea bushes all along the front steps. They bloomed this bright, vibrant pink every spring. Folks would come from two towns over just to take pictures in front of them for prom. I remember my own prom, I wore this awful powder blue tuxedo with a ruffled shirt. Ruffles! Can you imagine? My date was Mary Lou Jenkins. Sweet girl, but she stepped on my feet the whole night. We went to this little diner afterwards that used to be on the corner of Main and 4th. Best milkshakes in the county. They made them with real malt powder, not that artificial stuff they use nowadays. The guy who ran the place, Sal, he had this glass eye that always seemed to be looking just a little bit to the left of you when he talked...\n\nBut anyway, yeah. I think about it constantly. It reminds me of this old cast-iron anvil my grandfather used to keep in his barn. He was a blacksmith, one of the last real ones in the county. Used to shoe horses for all the local farms. That anvil must have weighed two hundred pounds easy. We kids used to try and lift it, make a game out of it, but nobody could budge the thing. Except my cousin Tommy. Tommy was built like a brick outhouse even when he was twelve. He grew up and joined the Navy, ended up stationed out in San Diego. Beautiful out there, they say. I've never been past Chicago myself. We took a train to Chicago once, back in '68, for a trade show. The sleeper cars were so cramped, and the food in the dining car was mostly just salty ham and these little boiled potatoes that tasted like they'd been in the pot since Tuesday... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The guilt, right. Yeah, I don't really know how to describe the intensity of it. It's just... it's always there, making me feel like I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you're afraid of having a panic attack or some other strong physical reaction if you encounter reminders of the argument?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's about a panic attack or... or a physical reaction, really. I'm just so tired of planning my whole life around not running into those reminders, you know? \n\nReminders... it's funny how things stick with you. Like the smell of sawdust. I used to love that smell. Reminds me of when I was a kid helping Mr. Henderson build birdhouses. Buster was the friendliest dog, except when the mailman came. The mailman back then was a fella named George. George always wore these little blue shorts, even in November. The radio only played country music, no matter what dial you turned...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just... I came in because I'm avoiding the workshop and the family things. I'm just exhausted from trying to dodge it all."
